=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given positive integers N, M, and a sequence A = (A_1, A_2, \dots, A_N) of length N, each element being an integer between 1 and N, inclusive.
Find the number, modulo 998244353, of sequences x = (x_1, x_2, \dots, x_N) of length N, each element being an integer between 1 and M, inclusive, that satisfy the following condition:

- x_i \leq x_{A_i} for every i (1 \leq i \leq N).

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
A_1 A_2 \dots A_N

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N, M \leq 2025
- 1 \leq A_i \leq N
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3 3
2 1 1

Sample Output 1

6

The sequences x=(1,1,1),(2,2,1),(2,2,2),(3,3,1),(3,3,2),(3,3,3) satisfy the condition.

Sample Input 2

4 9
1 1 1 1

Sample Output 2

2025

Sample Input 3

10 5
9 4 5 5 4 2 1 5 7 2

Sample Output 3

10010

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's explore memory-efficient DP in detail.

We have original graph with cycles. We can build children lists for all nodes (reverse edges). Identify in_cycle. Then we need compute prefix arrays for non-cycle nodes. - For each cycle SCC, initialize prod array of length M+1 with ones? Need product over attached roots prefix. For each non-cycle child u of any cycle node in SCC:
    pref = dfs(u)  # returns prefix array for root u
    multiply prod[k] *= pref[k] for k=1..M
    discard pref
  After all attached roots, comp_count = sum(prod[1..M]) mod. answer *= comp_count.

Need compute prefix_v from dp_v. We can write recursive function `dfs(v)` that returns prefix array for subtree rooted at v. It assumes v non-cycle. It will compute product of prefixes of children.

Pseudo:

MOD = 998244353

def dfs(v):
    # children = children[v] (non-cycle)
    if not children[v]:
        # prefix[k] = k mod MOD for k=0..M
        pref = [0]*(M+1)
        # set pref[k]=k
        for k in range(1,M+1):
            pref[k] = k % MOD  # M<=2025 so k
        return pref
    # Choose first child maybe largest subtree to reduce memory? Need know subtree sizes. Could just use first child; memory likely okay. But if we want robust, compute subtree sizes first for non-cycle forest.
    # Without sizes, recursion with reusing first child prefix and holding prod while recursing other children could accumulate many prods in worst-case. But N=2025, maybe okay? Let's analyze worst-case if no ordering: A tree where each node has two children: one leaf and one deep chain. If children list order puts leaf first, then dfs(v) sets prod = dfs(leaf) (prefix length M), then for second child deep chain, it holds prod while recursing deep chain. Inside deep chain, same: leaf first then deep, so each ancestor holds prod while recursing deep. This would allocate O(depth*M) memory, ~4M ints again. But still maybe okay. But if we want memory efficient, we can order children by subtree size descending so deep child first. Need compute subtree sizes for non-cycle nodes.

Alternative iterative bottom-up with prefix arrays for all nodes is simpler. Which approach to final code? Need choose robust and easy.

Let's evaluate constraints: N,M<=2025. O(N*M) time. Memory with all prefixes likely accepted. But final code should be concise. We can use list of lists and no recursion. Let's see if there is an even simpler O(N*M) with arrays of Python lists.

Implementation with pruning and DP:

```
import sys
MOD=998244353
def main():
    input=sys.stdin.readline
    N,M=map(int,input().split())
    A=[0]+list(map(int,input().split()))
    children=[[] for _ in range(N+1)]
    indeg=[0]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1,N+1):
        p=A[i]
        children[p].append(i)
        indeg[p]+=1
    from collections import deque
    q=deque([i for i in range(1,N+1) if indeg[i]==0])
    order=[]
    while q:
        v=q.popleft()
        order.append(v)
        p=A[v]
        indeg[p]-=1
        if indeg[p]==0:
            q.append(p)
    in_cycle=[False]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1,N+1):
        if indeg[i]>0:
            in_cycle[i]=True
    # dp_prefix[v] for non-cycle nodes
    pref=[None]*(N+1)
    for v in order:  # leaves to roots
        # compute dp_v[k] = product pref[child][k] for child in children[v] (all non-cycle)
        # Since v non-cycle, children[v] all non-cycle and processed.
        dp=[1]*(M+1)  # dp[0] unused maybe 1
        for u in children[v]:
            pu=pref[u]
            # multiply dp[k] *= pu[k]
            for k in range(1,M+1):
                dp[k] = dp[k] * pu[k] % MOD
        # compute prefix
        pref_v=[0]*(M+1)
        s=0
        for k in range(1,M+1):
            s += dp[k]
            if s >= MOD: s -= MOD? But dp[k] mod, sum up to M*MOD ~2e12, can do s = (s + dp[k]) % MOD
            pref_v[k]=s
        pref[v]=pref_v
    # group cycles
    visited_cycle=[False]*(N+1)
    ans=1
    for i in range(1,N+1):
        if in_cycle[i] and not visited_cycle[i]:
            # traverse cycle
            nodes=[]
            v=i
            while not visited_cycle[v]:
                visited_cycle[v]=True
                nodes.append(v)
                v=A[v]
            # product array for this SCC
            prod=[1]*(M+1)
            for c in nodes:
                for u in children[c]:
                    if not in_cycle[u]:
                        pu=pref[u]
                        for k in range(1,M+1):
                            prod[k] = prod[k] * pu[k] % MOD
            comp_count=sum(prod[1:])%MOD
            ans = ans * comp_count % MOD
    print(ans)
```

Need verify order processing for non-cycle nodes. `order` from pruning includes all non-cycle nodes? Yes, nodes removed are exactly non-cycle. For each v in order, children[v] may include nodes that are also in order and processed earlier? Need ensure. In pruning, a node is pushed when indegree becomes 0. For non-cycle v, indegree[v] counts all incoming edges from children (non-cycle) plus maybe self? If v non-cycle, no self-loop. It becomes 0 after all children removed. So v appears after all its children in order. Thus when processing order list from start to end, children processed before v. Good.

But there is subtlety: children[v] may include nodes in cycle? We argued no. Let's prove: If u in cycle and A[u]=v, then u's outgoing points to v. Since v non-cycle, following from u goes to v then eventually cycle, so u not on a directed cycle. Contradiction. So no.

Now, for cycle grouping, if we traverse `while not visited_cycle[v]: v=A[v]`, starting from a cycle node, will stay in cycle and eventually return to start. Since in_cycle true and A maps cycle to cycle. Good. But if we start at i in cycle not visited, following A will traverse the cycle; all nodes in that cycle get visited. Good.

Need compute comp_count for cycle SCC. We use `prod` initialized to 1 for all k. For each non-cycle child u of any cycle node, multiply prefix[u]. But wait: What about non-cycle child u whose parent is a cycle node c; prefix[u] is number of assignments of subtree rooted at u with root label <= t? Product over attached roots. Then sum over k. Good.

But what about cycle nodes themselves with children that are cycle nodes (next in cycle) and non-cycle. We ignore cycle children because equality already accounted. Good.

Let's test with a simple functional graph: N=2, M=2, A=[2,2]? Edges 1->2, 2->2 (self-loop). Cycle {2}, child 1. Count: x2=k, x1<=k. Sum k=1..2 k=3. Sequences: (1,1),(1,2),(2,2). N=2, M=2, A=[1,1]. Cycle {1}, child2. Count root x1=k, x2<=k: sum k=3. DP same.

Now, consider a non-cycle node with multiple children and M small. Example root cycle label k, node v child of cycle, v has two leaf children. Count for component: root label k, v label s<=k, leaves <=s. For each k, number = sum_{s=1}^k s^2. Total = sum_k sum_s s^2. DP: leaf prefix L[t]=t. For v: dp_v[s] = L[s]*L[s] = s^2; prefix_v[k]=sum_{s<=k}s^2. Root comp_count=sum_k prefix_v[k]. Good.

Our DP for non-cycle node v: children leaves, dp_v[k]=product pref_child[k] = k*k. prefix_v cumulative. Good.

Very fast.

Potential issue: `dp=[1]*(M+1)` for each node, then multiply. For leaf, dp all 1, prefix_v[k]=k. We can optimize leaf by directly creating prefix to avoid loop? But okay.

Let's think if `pref` list of lists with Python ints may be too slow due to memory? 4M operations okay. But allocating 2025 lists of length 2026, each with zeros and then ints, okay. Time maybe 0.1-0.5s? Actually Python 4M loops maybe 0.2s, but with list allocations and modulo maybe 1s. Fine.

Need ensure modulo for prefix sum: `s = (s + dp[k]) % MOD` inside loop. M=2025, okay. Could avoid modulo every addition by if s >= MOD: s -= MOD, but s+dp can be up to 2*MOD <2e9, so one subtraction enough. Since s maintained <MOD, dp<MOD, sum<2MOD. Use:
```
s += dp[k]
if s >= MOD: s -= MOD
pref_v[k]=s
```
This is faster. For product multiplication, `dp[k] = dp[k] * pu[k] % MOD`, product up to 1e18, Python handles. Good.

For comp_count sum(prod[1:]) % MOD: sum up to M*MOD ~2e12, okay; can do `%MOD`.

Let's refine implementation for speed and memory.

Potential issue with `pref = [None]*(N+1)` and storing arrays for all non-cycle nodes; when processing cycle comp, for each non-cycle child u, `pref[u]` exists. Good.

Memory-efficient iterative bottom-up without storing all prefixes? We can process nodes in `order` (leaves to roots) and store prefix for each node until parent processed. Since order is leaves to roots, when processing v, all children have been processed and their prefixes are in `pref`. But star with root non-cycle and many leaves: N=2025, M=2025, storing 2024 leaf prefixes ~4M ints. Same. However, we can free leaf prefixes after parent processed; peak still high. Recursive DFS with reusing first child prefix can reduce peak for star: process first leaf, get prefix, use as prod, process second leaf while holding prod, multiply, discard second, etc. Peak O(M) plus recursion. So recursive memory-efficient is better.

Let's design recursive memory-efficient solution carefully.

We have children lists for all nodes. Need identify in_cycle. Then for each cycle SCC, for each non-cycle child u of cycle node, call `dfs(u)` to get prefix array. `dfs(v)` returns prefix array for subtree rooted at v (v non-cycle). It computes product of `dfs(child)` for children[v] (all non-cycle). To reduce memory, we can order children by subtree size descending and process largest first as first child. But computing subtree sizes requires an initial DFS. N=2025, we can compute sizes easily. But maybe not necessary for correctness, only memory. If we don't order, worst-case memory O(N*M) but still maybe okay. But if we want memory-efficient, let's implement with child ordering by subtree size.

Need compute subtree sizes for non-cycle forest. We can do iterative postorder from roots (non-cycle nodes whose parent in cycle) using children. Since N small, simple recursion for sizes too. Or use `order` from pruning: order is leaves to roots. We can compute size[v] = 1 + sum size[child] for children[v] by iterating reversed(order)? Wait order is leaves to roots, so reversed(order) is roots to leaves? Let's see order [leaf, ..., root]. If we iterate reversed(order), root first, children not computed. So need iterate order (leaves first) to compute sizes? For size, size[v] = 1 + sum size[child]. Children appear before v in order, so iterating order forward works: initialize size[v]=1 for all; for v in order: for u in children[v]: size[v] += size[u]? But children already have size computed. Yes. Or simpler: size[v]=1; for v in order (leaves to roots), after processing v, add size[v] to size[parent] if parent non-cycle. But children lists okay.

Actually order from pruning is leaves to roots. We can compute `subsize[v]=1` for all non-cycle; for v in order: for u in children[v]: subsize[v] += subsize[u] (children processed). Good.

Then for each non-cycle node, sort children[v] by subsize descending. We can do after sizes computed. But sorting all children lists total O(N log N) small.

Now recursive `dfs(v)`:
- If no children: return prefix array for leaf: pref[k]=k mod M. Since M<=2025, k.
- Else:
   Let children sorted descending.
   # Compute first child prefix and use as prod (dp_v)
   prod = dfs(children[0])
   # For each other child:
   for u in children[1:]:
       child_pref = dfs(u)
       for k in range(1, M+1):
           prod[k] = prod[k] * child_pref[k] % MOD
       # child_pref can be discarded
   # Now prod[k] = dp_v[k] = product over children prefix_child[k]
   # Convert prod in-place to prefix_v:
   s = 0
   for k in range(1, M+1):
       s += prod[k]
       if s >= MOD: s -= MOD
       prod[k] = s
   return prod

Wait: If we use first child's prefix array as `prod`, and then multiply other child prefixes into it, we are modifying the first child's prefix. That's fine because first child's prefix is no longer needed by its parent? It is needed by current node only. But what about if first child prefix is also needed by another parent? No, each node has one parent. So safe. However, after converting prod to prefix_v, we are modifying the array that was first child's prefix. That's okay. But if first child is also a root attached to cycle? No, if v is parent, first child's parent is v, so not directly attached to cycle. So no other use.

But there is a subtlety: If we process first child and get its prefix, then use it as prod. Then when processing second child, `prod` is the first child's prefix array. We multiply `prod[k] *= child_pref[k]`. This destroys first child prefix. Fine. But what if first child's prefix array is needed for computing `prod` for v's parent? No, v's parent needs v's prefix, not child's. So fine.

Potential issue: If `children[v]` has one child, prod = dfs(child) returns child's prefix. Then we convert it in-place to prefix_v. This modifies child prefix to become v prefix. That's okay. But if child prefix was also stored elsewhere? No. This means we don't allocate new array for v; we reuse child's array. Memory efficient.

But wait: For a node v with multiple children, after multiplying all children into first child's prefix array, we convert to prefix_v. The first child's prefix array is used as dp_v. But dp_v[k] initially should be 1 for all k if no children; with children, product of child prefixes. Since first child prefix is not 1, but product over children including first child is correct: prod starts as prefix_first, then multiply others. Good.

Now, what about a node with children but first child prefix array length M+1 and prod[0] maybe prefix_first[0]=0. We only use k=1..M. After conversion, prefix_v[0] remains 0. Good.

Now, if a node has no children, we create prefix array with pref[k]=k. But if we use this array as prod for parent, parent may multiply other child prefixes into it. That's fine.

Now, cycle root count: For each cycle SCC, we need product over attached roots' prefix arrays. We can similarly use first attached root's prefix as `prod` to avoid allocation:
```

else:

```

However, if we call dfs(u) for attached root, it may reuse arrays from its subtree. No global storage. Good.

Need ensure each non-cycle tree is processed exactly once. We call dfs on each non-cycle child of cycle nodes. Since every non-cycle node has exactly one parent; if parent is cycle, it's a root. If parent non-cycle, it's inside some root's tree. So calling dfs on roots covers all non-cycle nodes. Good.

But recursive function with child sorting by subtree size: To compute subsize, we can use `order` from pruning. Let's integrate.

Now, let's think about recursion and reusing arrays with sorting. Suppose a node v has children c1 (large) and c2 (small). dfs(v):
- prod = dfs(c1) returns prefix_c1 array.

Let's test a worst-case for memory with this recursion: Root has two children: large child A (size 1000) and small child B (size 1000? equal). If equal, process A first, prod allocated after A returns, then process B while holding prod. Inside B, it processes its children similarly. At each level, if children sizes equal, one prod allocated per level? Let's analyze: Root prod allocated while dfs(B). Inside B, after its first child returns, it allocates prod_B while processing its second child. So memory includes root prod + prod_B + ... along path in B. Since B size halves? If root children equal, B size ~1000, root prod allocated. Inside B, if its children equal, prod_B allocated while processing second child of size ~500. So memory O(M * depth of equal splits) = O(M log N) because sizes halve. Good. If children sizes not equal but first largest, any non-first child size <= (parent_size-1)/2? If first largest m, second s<=m, parent size =1+sum children >=1+m+s >=1+2s? Since m>=s, parent_size >=1+m+s >=1+2s, so s <= (parent_size-1)/2. Thus each time we descend into a non-first child, size halves. Along a path, number of non-first descents O(log N). At each such descent, an ancestor prod is on stack. So memory O(M log N) plus recursion stack. Good.

But wait, if a node has only one child, no prod allocated before recursing into that child (since first child computed before prod). So chain memory O(M). Good.

Thus memory-efficient recursion is robust.

Now, let's think about modular arithmetic for prefix leaf: `pref[k] = k % MOD`, but M<=2025 < MOD, so k. We can create `pref = list(range(M+1))`? Now, one issue: In `dfs(v)`, if v has children, we set `prod = dfs(children[0])`. This returns a prefix array for first child. Then for other children, `child_pref = dfs(u)`. If `u` is leaf, `dfs(u)` returns `list(range(M+1))`. We multiply into `prod` and then discard. Good.

Now, let's think about sorting children by subsize. We compute subsize for non-cycle nodes. But if a node has children that are cycle nodes? Non-cycle node cannot. How to sort attached roots per cycle? We can collect `roots` list for each cycle: for c in nodes, for u in children[c] if not in_cycle[u], append u. Then sort roots by subsize[u] descending. Then process:
```
prod = None
for u in roots:
    pref_u = dfs(u)
    if prod is None:

    else:
        multiply
```
This ensures largest root processed first, so when processing subsequent smaller roots, prod allocated but root size <= half? Now, computing subsize for non-cycle nodes: We can also use it to sort children[v]. For cycle nodes, subsize not set, but roots are non-cycle so subsize set.

Let's write code for memory-efficient version.

Pseudo code:

```
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(10000)
MOD=998244353

def main():
    input=sys.stdin.readline
    N,M=map(int,input().split())
    A=[0]+list(map(int,input().split()))
    children=[[] for _ in range(N+1)]
    indeg=[0]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1,N+1):
        p=A[i]
        children[p].append(i)
        indeg[p]+=1

    from collections import deque
    q=deque([i for i in range(1,N+1) if indeg[i]==0])
    order=[]
    while q:
        v=q.popleft()
        order.append(v)
        p=A[v]
        indeg[p]-=1
        if indeg[p]==0:
            q.append(p)

    in_cycle=[False]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1,N+1):
        if indeg[i]>0:
            in_cycle[i]=True

    subsize=[0]*(N+1)
    for v in order:
        sz=1
        for u in children[v]:
            sz += subsize[u]
        subsize[v]=sz

    for v in range(1,N+1):
        if not in_cycle[v] and len(children[v])>1:
            children[v].sort(key=lambda u: subsize[u], reverse=True)

    def dfs(v):
        if not children[v]:
            return list(range(M+1))
        prod = dfs(children[v][0])
        for u in children[v][1:]:
            pref_u = dfs(u)
            # multiply
            for k in range(1, M+1):
                prod[k] = (prod[k] * pref_u[k]) % MOD
        # convert prod to prefix
        s=0
        for k in range(1, M+1):
            s += prod[k]
            if s >= MOD:
                s -= MOD
            prod[k] = s
        return prod

    visited_cycle=[False]*(N+1)
    ans=1
    for i in range(1,N+1):
        if in_cycle[i] and not visited_cycle[i]:
            nodes=[]
            v=i
            while not visited_cycle[v]:
                visited_cycle[v]=True
                nodes.append(v)
                v=A[v]
            roots=[]
            for c in nodes:
                for u in children[c]:
                    if not in_cycle[u]:
                        roots.append(u)
            roots.sort(key=lambda u: subsize[u], reverse=True)
            prod=None
            for u in roots:
                pref_u=dfs(u)
                if prod is None:
                    prod=pref_u
                else:
                    for k in range(1,M+1):
                        prod[k] = (prod[k] * pref_u[k]) % MOD
            if prod is None:
                comp_count = M % MOD
            else:
                comp_count = sum(prod[1:]) % MOD
            ans = ans * comp_count % MOD
    print(ans)
```

Need check `children[v]` for non-cycle v after sorting: If `children[v]` includes cycle nodes? No. But if v non-cycle, children[v] all non-cycle. Good.

Potential issue: In `dfs`, if `children[v]` is not empty but all children? yes.

Good.

Sample2:
- order [2,3,4]? Actually indeg [1]=4, others 0. q initially [2,3,4]. Pop 2, indeg1=3; pop3, indeg1=2; pop4, indeg1=1. order [2,3,4]. subsize each 1. children[1] cycle not sorted. cycle {1}, roots [2,3,4] sorted all subsize 1. dfs(2) leaf returns [0..9]; prod=that. dfs(3) leaf returns [0..9]; multiply prod[k]*=k -> k^2. dfs(4) -> k^3. comp_count=sum k^3=2025. Good.

for v in order:

Let's write final code carefully:

```
import sys
from collections import deque

MOD = 998244353

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N, M = map(int, input().split())
    A = [0] + list(map(int, input().split()))
    children = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    indeg = [0] * (N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        p = A[i]
        children[p].append(i)
        indeg[p] += 1

    q = deque([i for i in range(1, N + 1) if indeg[i] == 0])
    order = []
    while q:
        v = q.popleft()
        order.append(v)
        p = A[v]
        indeg[p] -= 1
        if indeg[p] == 0:
            q.append(p)

    in_cycle = [False] * (N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        if indeg[i] > 0:
            in_cycle[i] = True

    subsize = [0] * (N + 1)
    for v in order:
        sz = 1
        for u in children[v]:
            sz += subsize[u]
        subsize[v] = sz

    for v in range(1, N + 1):
        if not in_cycle[v] and len(children[v]) > 1:
            children[v].sort(key=lambda u: subsize[u], reverse=True)

    sys.setrecursionlimit(10000)

    def dfs(v):
        if not children[v]:
            return list(range(M + 1))
        prod = dfs(children[v][0])
        for u in children[v][1:]:
            pref = dfs(u)
            # multiply prod by pref
            for k in range(1, M + 1):
                prod[k] = (prod[k] * pref[k]) % MOD
        s = 0
        for k in range(1, M + 1):
            s += prod[k]
            if s >= MOD:
                s -= MOD
            prod[k] = s
        return prod

    visited_cycle = [False] * (N + 1)
    ans = 1
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        if in_cycle[i] and not visited_cycle[i]:
            nodes = []
            v = i
            while not visited_cycle[v]:
                visited_cycle[v] = True
                nodes.append(v)
                v = A[v]
            roots = []
            for c in nodes:
                for u in children[c]:
                    if not in_cycle[u]:
                        roots.append(u)
            roots.sort(key=lambda u: subsize[u], reverse=True)
            prod = None
            for u in roots:
                pref = dfs(u)
                if prod is None:
                    prod = pref
                else:
                    for k in range(1, M + 1):
                        prod[k] = (prod[k] * pref[k]) % MOD
            if prod is None:
                comp = M % MOD
            else:
                comp = sum(prod) % MOD
            ans = ans * comp % MOD

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Need check `sys.setrecursionlimit` placement: It should be before dfs definition or before calls. We set before definition. Good.

No, tree no duplicate children. Could a child's dfs return an array that was previously used as prod for an ancestor and then somehow passed down? No, arrays are returned upward, not downward. So no alias.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code if the modulo multiplication in dfs uses `% MOD` for each k; if prod[k] and pref[k] are ints, okay. Could optimize by localizing `mod = MOD` and `m = M` to avoid global lookups. Let's write code with local variables for speed:

```
    mod = MOD
    m = M
    def dfs(v):
        if not children[v]:
            return list(range(m + 1))
        ch = children[v]
        prod = dfs(ch[0])
        for idx in range(1, len(ch)):
            pref = dfs(ch[idx])
            for k in range(1, m + 1):
                prod[k] = (prod[k] * pref[k]) % mod
        s = 0
        for k in range(1, m + 1):
            s += prod[k]
            if s >= mod:
                s -= mod
            prod[k] = s
        return prod
```
Use `m` and `mod` local. Good.

Similarly in cycle comp:
```
            for k in range(1, m + 1):

```

In a loop with many children, after processing a child, `pref` still references the child array until next iteration assigns a new `pref` or function ends. This means during the next child's `dfs`, the previous child's array is still referenced by `pref`, so memory includes prod + previous child's array + current child's recursion. Let's consider: In `dfs`, after multiplying `pref` into `prod`, `pref` remains referenced until the next iteration. If the next child is large, memory includes previous child's prefix array (which could be large) plus prod plus current recursion. This could increase memory. We can `del pref` at end of loop to free before recursing into next child. Similarly in cycle comp. Let's add `del pref` after multiplication to reduce memory. But Python may not free immediately? It decrements ref count, memory available. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
.</think>```python
import sys
from collections import deque

MOD = 998244353

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    A = [0] + data[2:2 + N]

    children = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    indeg = [0] * (N + 1)

    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        p = A[i]
        children[p].append(i)
        indeg[p] += 1

    # Remove non-cycle nodes.
    q = deque([i for i in range(1, N + 1) if indeg[i] == 0])
    order = []

    while q:
        v = q.popleft()
        order.append(v)
        p = A[v]
        indeg[p] -= 1
        if indeg[p] == 0:
            q.append(p)

    in_cycle = [False] * (N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        if indeg[i] > 0:
            in_cycle[i] = True

    # Subtree sizes in the forest of non-cycle nodes.
    subsize = [0] * (N + 1)
    for v in order:
        s = 1
        for u in children[v]:
            s += subsize[u]
        subsize[v] = s

    # Process larger subtrees first to keep memory small.
    for v in range(1, N + 1):
        if not in_cycle[v] and len(children[v]) > 1:
            children[v].sort(key=lambda u: subsize[u], reverse=True)

    sys.setrecursionlimit(10000)

    def dfs(v):
        """Return prefix array of subtree rooted at non-cycle node v."""
        if not children[v]:
            return list(range(M + 1))

        ch = children[v]
        prod = dfs(ch[0])

        for idx in range(1, len(ch)):
            pref = dfs(ch[idx])
            for k in range(1, M + 1):
                prod[k] = (prod[k] * pref[k]) % MOD
            del pref

        # Convert dp values to prefix sums.
        s = 0
        for k in range(1, M + 1):
            s += prod[k]
            if s >= MOD:
                s -= MOD
            prod[k] = s

        return prod

    visited_cycle = [False] * (N + 1)
    ans = 1

    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        if not in_cycle[i] or visited_cycle[i]:
            continue

        # Collect one directed cycle.
        nodes = []
        v = i
        while not visited_cycle[v]:
            visited_cycle[v] = True
            nodes.append(v)
            v = A[v]

        # Roots are non-cycle children of cycle nodes.
        roots = []
        for c in nodes:
            for u in children[c]:
                if not in_cycle[u]:
                    roots.append(u)

        roots.sort(key=lambda u: subsize[u], reverse=True)

        prod = None
        for r in roots:
            pref = dfs(r)
            if prod is None:
                prod = pref
            else:
                for k in range(1, M + 1):
                    prod[k] = (prod[k] * pref[k]) % MOD
                del pref

        if prod is None:
            comp_count = M % MOD
        else:
            comp_count = sum(prod) % MOD

        ans = ans * comp_count % MOD

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
